I’m not sure. I don’t think of myself as someone who can’t start things, exactly. If anything, once I know what I’m doing, I can be pretty… diligent.

I guess it does, yes… though the main place I’ve noticed it is work, because that’s where the consequences are most obvious.

Like, I came in because I can’t really keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I’ll notice something small—a pump vibration, or a student making some side comment, or some odd peak in an old spectrum—and then suddenly I’m off chasing that for hours. And the thing everyone actually needed from me just… doesn’t get done, or it falls apart.

I don’t mean I’m trying to be careless. It feels like my attention gets pulled so hard by the detail that I almost convince myself it’s important. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of “daily life” effect you mean, but it’s definitely affecting what I’m responsible for.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because work has gotten… harder to manage than I want to admit. Meanwhile the thing people actually need from me just sort of… falls apart.\n\nI know that probably sounds like normal distraction, and maybe I’m making too much of it, but it’s been causing enough problems that I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having thoughts or ideas that keep coming back, even if you don’t want them to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not in the sense of, like, a thought I’m trying to push away and it keeps intruding? I mean, I do get very caught on ideas sometimes, but I usually experience it as me following a thread rather than something unwanted.\n\nMaybe I’m splitting hairs. If you mean intrusive thoughts, I don’t think that’s really what’s happening. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose track of time when you're deeply focused on something that interests you, even if it's not directly related to your main task?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I mean, I wouldn’t have called it “losing track of time” at first, because it feels productive in the moment. Like I’m noticing something important.\n\nBut I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and something small catches my attention—a pump vibration, or a student’s offhand comment, or some strange peak in an old spectrum—and then I’m suddenly off following that for hours. And the actual experiment, or the thing people were waiting on from me, just… doesn’t get done the way it should.\n\nI know that probably sounds a bit dramatic, but yes, that’s been happening more than I’m comfortable with.\n\nDoctor: Do you often become so deeply absorbed in specific interests or details that you lose awareness of what's going on around you, even when those details aren't directly related to the task at hand?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. I don’t mean to tune everything else out, and it doesn’t feel careless when it’s happening. It feels like I’ve noticed something that might matter.\n\nBut then, yes, I’ll get completely absorbed in some detail that isn’t really the main task—like a pump vibration during a meeting, or a student’s side comment, or an odd peak in an old spectrum—and suddenly I’ve spent hours chasing that. And by then the actual experiment, or whatever people were waiting on from me, has kind of fallen apart.\n\nI know that probably sounds like I’m just being disorganized, but it’s been more than I can comfortably brush off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to start tasks or keep them organized, even when you're not following a distracting detail?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think of myself as someone who can’t start things, exactly. If anything, once I know what I’m doing, I can be pretty… diligent.\n\nBut organizing it all can get messy sometimes, yes. I’m hesitant to overstate that, because I don’t want it to sound like I’m just unreliable. It’s more that if there are too many steps or loose ends, I can spend a long time trying to decide the “right” order to do them in, or tidying the plan instead of actually beginning.\n\nI don’t know if that’s what you mean, though. Is that the kind of difficulty you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to follow conversations or pick up on social cues when interacting with others, especially in settings that aren't related to your work or specific interests?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least, not in an obvious way. I mean, I can follow conversations, especially if I understand the context or there’s some purpose to it.\n\nSocial cues… I’m not sure. I might miss things occasionally, but I don’t know that it’s more than anyone else does. Outside of work I can feel a bit less sure of myself, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it or make it sound like I can’t interact with people. If there’s something specific you mean by “social cues,” I can try to answer that better.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your experiences, do you recall having difficulty connecting with peers or making friends growing up, or feeling like you related to others differently, especially in situations not related to shared interests or work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as difficulty making friends, exactly. I had friends, at least at different points. I wasn’t completely isolated or anything.\n\nI do remember sometimes feeling a little… out of step, maybe. Like I was better when there was a shared class project or a topic we were all focused on, and less sure what to do in more casual situations. But I don’t know if that was really unusual, or just being a bit shy or serious.\n\nI wouldn’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it was. I guess I’m not sure how to judge that looking back.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if you often felt more comfortable engaging with others only when there was a shared activity or specific topic, rather than in more open or casual social situations?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe sometimes, yes, but I’m not sure I’d say “only.” That sounds a little stronger than how I remember it.\n\nI was probably more comfortable when there was something to do or talk about—like a class, a project, a specific subject—because then I knew what was expected of me. Casual situations could feel more vague, I guess. But I don’t know if that was unusual, or just that I liked having some structure.\n\nDoes that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these difficulties with focus or getting absorbed in details also affect your daily life outside of work, such as managing household tasks, relationships, or personal responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I guess it does, yes… though the main place I’ve noticed it is work, because that’s where the consequences are most obvious.\n\nLike, I came in because I can’t really keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I’ll notice something small—a pump vibration, or a student making some side comment, or some odd peak in an old spectrum—and then suddenly I’m off chasing that for hours. And the thing everyone actually needed from me just… doesn’t get done, or it falls apart.\n\nI don’t mean I’m trying to be careless. It feels like my attention gets pulled so hard by the detail that I almost convince myself it’s important. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of “daily life” effect you mean, but it’s definitely affecting what I’m responsible for.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you forget everyday things, struggle to manage your time, or have trouble keeping up with personal responsibilities like chores, appointments, or relationships, even when you’re not at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably does, though I notice it most clearly in work situations.\n\nIt’s less like I just forget a chore outright, and more like my mind gets pulled away from what I’m supposed to be doing. Like I said, I came in because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, notice something small—a pump vibration, a student’s side comment, some odd peak in an old spectrum—and then I’m off chasing that for hours. Meanwhile the thing people actually needed from me just falls apart.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s still a work example. It’s just the cleanest one I can explain.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think it goes all the way back to childhood, no. At least, not in any way I can clearly remember.

This has been more recent… about seven months, I think. It started after I took over the multi-site spectroscopy project. I don’t want to make it sound like I was perfectly organized before that, but this particular losing time and getting pulled into details really became noticeable around then.

If you mean intrusive thoughts, I don’t think that’s really what’s happening. And the actual experiment, or the thing people were waiting on from me, just… doesn’t get done the way it should.\n\nI know that probably sounds a bit dramatic, but yes, that’s been happening more than I’m comfortable with.\n\nDoctor: Do you often become so deeply absorbed in specific interests or details that you lose awareness of what's going on around you, even when those details aren't directly related to the task at hand?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. I don’t mean to tune everything else out, and it doesn’t feel careless when it’s happening. It feels like I’ve noticed something that might matter.\n\nBut then, yes, I’ll get completely absorbed in some detail that isn’t really the main task—like a pump vibration during a meeting, or a student’s side comment, or an odd peak in an old spectrum—and suddenly I’ve spent hours chasing that. And by then the actual experiment, or whatever people were waiting on from me, has kind of fallen apart.\n\nI know that probably sounds like I’m just being disorganized, but it’s been more than I can comfortably brush off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to start tasks or keep them organized, even when you're not following a distracting detail?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think of myself as someone who can’t start things, exactly. If anything, once I know what I’m doing, I can be pretty… diligent.\n\nBut organizing it all can get messy sometimes, yes. I’m hesitant to overstate that, because I don’t want it to sound like I’m just unreliable. It’s more that if there are too many steps or loose ends, I can spend a long time trying to decide the “right” order to do them in, or tidying the plan instead of actually beginning.\n\nI don’t know if that’s what you mean, though. Is that the kind of difficulty you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to follow conversations or pick up on social cues when interacting with others, especially in settings that aren't related to your work or specific interests?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least, not in an obvious way. I mean, I can follow conversations, especially if I understand the context or there’s some purpose to it.\n\nSocial cues… I’m not sure. I might miss things occasionally, but I don’t know that it’s more than anyone else does. Outside of work I can feel a bit less sure of myself, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it or make it sound like I can’t interact with people. If there’s something specific you mean by “social cues,” I can try to answer that better.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your experiences, do you recall having difficulty connecting with peers or making friends growing up, or feeling like you related to others differently, especially in situations not related to shared interests or work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as difficulty making friends, exactly. I had friends, at least at different points. I wasn’t completely isolated or anything.\n\nI do remember sometimes feeling a little… out of step, maybe. Like I was better when there was a shared class project or a topic we were all focused on, and less sure what to do in more casual situations. But I don’t know if that was really unusual, or just being a bit shy or serious.\n\nI wouldn’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it was. I guess I’m not sure how to judge that looking back.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if you often felt more comfortable engaging with others only when there was a shared activity or specific topic, rather than in more open or casual social situations?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe sometimes, yes, but I’m not sure I’d say “only.” That sounds a little stronger than how I remember it.\n\nI was probably more comfortable when there was something to do or talk about—like a class, a project, a specific subject—because then I knew what was expected of me. Casual situations could feel more vague, I guess. But I don’t know if that was unusual, or just that I liked having some structure.\n\nDoes that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these difficulties with focus or getting absorbed in details also affect your daily life outside of work, such as managing household tasks, relationships, or personal responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I guess it does, yes… though the main place I’ve noticed it is work, because that’s where the consequences are most obvious.\n\nLike, I came in because I can’t really keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. At least, not in any way I can clearly remember.\n\nThis has been more recent… about seven months, I think. It started after I took over the multi-site spectroscopy project. I don’t want to make it sound like I was perfectly organized before that, but this particular losing time and getting pulled into details really became noticeable around then.\n\nIs that the kind of timeline you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you describe any situations outside of work where these attention issues have caused problems—like in personal relationships, hobbies, or other activities that used to be important to you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 42 / physical_scientist / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Manic_Distractibility\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I can't keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore—during a grant call or safety meeting I'll notice a pump vibration, a student's side comment, or some odd peak in an old spectrum and suddenly I'm off chasing that for hours while the thing everyone needs from me falls apart.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m trying to think of a good example outside of work, and I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is… but honestly the clearest examples are still tied to work.

It’s like during a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll catch some tiny thing—a pump vibration, a student’s side comment, or an odd peak in an old spectrum—and then my brain just locks onto it. I’ll follow that thread for hours, and meanwhile the main thing I was supposed to be doing, the thing people were counting on, sort of falls apart.

Outside work, I suppose the same pattern can bleed into personal time, because I’m still mentally chasing those details instead of being fully present. But I’m not sure I can give you a clean personal example without overstating it. Is that okay?

And by then the actual experiment, or whatever people were waiting on from me, has kind of fallen apart.\n\nI know that probably sounds like I’m just being disorganized, but it’s been more than I can comfortably brush off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to start tasks or keep them organized, even when you're not following a distracting detail?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think of myself as someone who can’t start things, exactly. If anything, once I know what I’m doing, I can be pretty… diligent.\n\nBut organizing it all can get messy sometimes, yes. I’m hesitant to overstate that, because I don’t want it to sound like I’m just unreliable. It’s more that if there are too many steps or loose ends, I can spend a long time trying to decide the “right” order to do them in, or tidying the plan instead of actually beginning.\n\nI don’t know if that’s what you mean, though. At least, not in any way I can clearly remember.\n\nThis has been more recent… about seven months, I think. It started after I took over the multi-site spectroscopy project. I don’t want to make it sound like I was perfectly organized before that, but this particular losing time and getting pulled into details really became noticeable around then.\n\nIs that the kind of timeline you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe any situations outside of work where these attention issues have caused problems—like in personal relationships, hobbies, or other activities that used to be important to you?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a good example outside of work, and I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is… but honestly the clearest examples are still tied to work.\n\nIt’s like during a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll catch some tiny thing—a pump vibration, a student’s side comment, or an odd peak in an old spectrum—and then my brain just locks onto it. I’ll follow that thread for hours, and meanwhile the main thing I was supposed to be doing, the thing people were counting on, sort of falls apart.\n\nOutside work, I suppose the same pattern can bleed into personal time, because I’m still mentally chasing those details instead of being fully present. But I’m not sure I can give you a clean personal example without overstating it. Is that okay?\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you describe a time when these attention issues interfered with your ability to maintain a conversation, complete a personal obligation, or interact effectively with someone close to you, even when work wasn’t involved?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m trying to think of one that isn’t really work-related, because I know you’re asking about people close to me or personal obligations.

The honest answer is… the strongest examples still happen around those meetings or calls. Like, I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and I should be following what someone is saying, but then I notice something tiny—a pump vibration, or a student making some side comment, or I remember an odd peak from an old spectrum—and suddenly I’m just gone mentally. I’ll chase that thread for hours afterward, and the actual conversation or obligation, the thing people needed me to stay with, just sort of falls apart.

I don’t mean that I’m trying to ignore anyone. It’s more like my attention gets yanked away before I’ve really decided to let it happen. I know that’s not a perfect personal example, but it is the way it interferes with interacting with people. Does that answer what you mean?

If you mean intrusive thoughts, I don’t think that’s really what’s happening. Like I’m noticing something important.\n\nBut I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and something small catches my attention—a pump vibration, or a student’s offhand comment, or some strange peak in an old spectrum—and then I’m suddenly off following that for hours. And the actual experiment, or the thing people were waiting on from me, just… doesn’t get done the way it should.\n\nI know that probably sounds a bit dramatic, but yes, that’s been happening more than I’m comfortable with.\n\nDoctor: Do you often become so deeply absorbed in specific interests or details that you lose awareness of what's going on around you, even when those details aren't directly related to the task at hand?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. I don’t mean to tune everything else out, and it doesn’t feel careless when it’s happening. It feels like I’ve noticed something that might matter.\n\nBut then, yes, I’ll get completely absorbed in some detail that isn’t really the main task—like a pump vibration during a meeting, or a student’s side comment, or an odd peak in an old spectrum—and suddenly I’ve spent hours chasing that. And by then the actual experiment, or whatever people were waiting on from me, has kind of fallen apart.\n\nI know that probably sounds like I’m just being disorganized, but it’s been more than I can comfortably brush off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to start tasks or keep them organized, even when you're not following a distracting detail?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think of myself as someone who can’t start things, exactly. If anything, once I know what I’m doing, I can be pretty… diligent.\n\nBut organizing it all can get messy sometimes, yes. I’m hesitant to overstate that, because I don’t want it to sound like I’m just unreliable. It’s more that if there are too many steps or loose ends, I can spend a long time trying to decide the “right” order to do them in, or tidying the plan instead of actually beginning.\n\nI don’t know if that’s what you mean, though. Is that the kind of difficulty you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to follow conversations or pick up on social cues when interacting with others, especially in settings that aren't related to your work or specific interests?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least, not in an obvious way. I mean, I can follow conversations, especially if I understand the context or there’s some purpose to it.\n\nSocial cues… I’m not sure. I might miss things occasionally, but I don’t know that it’s more than anyone else does. Outside of work I can feel a bit less sure of myself, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it or make it sound like I can’t interact with people. If there’s something specific you mean by “social cues,” I can try to answer that better.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your experiences, do you recall having difficulty connecting with peers or making friends growing up, or feeling like you related to others differently, especially in situations not related to shared interests or work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as difficulty making friends, exactly. I had friends, at least at different points. I wasn’t completely isolated or anything.\n\nI do remember sometimes feeling a little… out of step, maybe. Like I was better when there was a shared class project or a topic we were all focused on, and less sure what to do in more casual situations. But I don’t know if that was really unusual, or just being a bit shy or serious.\n\nI wouldn’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it was. I guess I’m not sure how to judge that looking back.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if you often felt more comfortable engaging with others only when there was a shared activity or specific topic, rather than in more open or casual social situations?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe sometimes, yes, but I’m not sure I’d say “only.” That sounds a little stronger than how I remember it.\n\nI was probably more comfortable when there was something to do or talk about—like a class, a project, a specific subject—because then I knew what was expected of me. Casual situations could feel more vague, I guess. But I don’t know if that was unusual, or just that I liked having some structure.\n\nDoes that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these difficulties with focus or getting absorbed in details also affect your daily life outside of work, such as managing household tasks, relationships, or personal responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I guess it does, yes… though the main place I’ve noticed it is work, because that’s where the consequences are most obvious.\n\nLike, I came in because I can’t really keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I’ll notice something small—a pump vibration, or a student making some side comment, or some odd peak in an old spectrum—and then suddenly I’m off chasing that for hours. It feels like my attention gets pulled so hard by the detail that I almost convince myself it’s important. I’m sorry, I know you’re asking outside of work, and I don’t want to dodge the question. I think… the problem is that the examples that feel “real” all start at work and then sort of take over everything else.

It’s more that if there are too many steps or loose ends, I can spend a long time trying to decide the “right” order to do them in, or tidying the plan instead of actually beginning.\n\nI don’t know if that’s what you mean, though. Is that the kind of difficulty you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to follow conversations or pick up on social cues when interacting with others, especially in settings that aren't related to your work or specific interests?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least, not in an obvious way. I mean, I can follow conversations, especially if I understand the context or there’s some purpose to it.\n\nSocial cues… I’m not sure. I might miss things occasionally, but I don’t know that it’s more than anyone else does. Outside of work I can feel a bit less sure of myself, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it or make it sound like I can’t interact with people. If there’s something specific you mean by “social cues,” I can try to answer that better.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your experiences, do you recall having difficulty connecting with peers or making friends growing up, or feeling like you related to others differently, especially in situations not related to shared interests or work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as difficulty making friends, exactly. I had friends, at least at different points. I wasn’t completely isolated or anything.\n\nI do remember sometimes feeling a little… out of step, maybe. Like I was better when there was a shared class project or a topic we were all focused on, and less sure what to do in more casual situations. But I don’t know if that was really unusual, or just being a bit shy or serious.\n\nI wouldn’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it was. I guess I’m not sure how to judge that looking back.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if you often felt more comfortable engaging with others only when there was a shared activity or specific topic, rather than in more open or casual social situations?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe sometimes, yes, but I’m not sure I’d say “only.” That sounds a little stronger than how I remember it.\n\nI was probably more comfortable when there was something to do or talk about—like a class, a project, a specific subject—because then I knew what was expected of me. Casual situations could feel more vague, I guess. But I don’t know if that was unusual, or just that I liked having some structure.\n\nDoes that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these difficulties with focus or getting absorbed in details also affect your daily life outside of work, such as managing household tasks, relationships, or personal responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I guess it does, yes… though the main place I’ve noticed it is work, because that’s where the consequences are most obvious.\n\nLike, I came in because I can’t really keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I’ll notice something small—a pump vibration, or a student making some side comment, or some odd peak in an old spectrum—and then suddenly I’m off chasing that for hours. And the thing everyone actually needed from me just… doesn’t get done, or it falls apart.\n\nI don’t mean I’m trying to be careless. It feels like my attention gets pulled so hard by the detail that I almost convince myself it’s important. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of “daily life” effect you mean, but it’s definitely affecting what I’m responsible for.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you forget everyday things, struggle to manage your time, or have trouble keeping up with personal responsibilities like chores, appointments, or relationships, even when you’re not at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably does, though I notice it most clearly in work situations.\n\nIt’s less like I just forget a chore outright, and more like my mind gets pulled away from what I’m supposed to be doing. Like I said, I came in because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, notice something small—a pump vibration, a student’s side comment, some odd peak in an old spectrum—and then I’m off chasing that for hours. Meanwhile the thing people actually needed from me just falls apart.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s still a work example. It’s just the cleanest one I can explain. I guess the same kind of thing can make me lose track of normal responsibilities too, but I don’t want to overstate it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Looking back, would you say these tendencies to lose track of time, get absorbed in details, and have trouble staying on task started early in your life—like in childhood—or have they mostly developed more recently, in adulthood?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it goes all the way back to childhood, no. At least, not in any way I can clearly remember.\n\nThis has been more recent… about seven months, I think. It started after I took over the multi-site spectroscopy project. I don’t want to make it sound like I was perfectly organized before that, but this particular losing time and getting pulled into details really became noticeable around then.\n\nIs that the kind of timeline you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe any situations outside of work where these attention issues have caused problems—like in personal relationships, hobbies, or other activities that used to be important to you?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a good example outside of work, and I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is… but honestly the clearest examples are still tied to work.\n\nIt’s like during a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll catch some tiny thing—a pump vibration, a student’s side comment, or an odd peak in an old spectrum—and then my brain just locks onto it. I’ll follow that thread for hours, and meanwhile the main thing I was supposed to be doing, the thing people were counting on, sort of falls apart.\n\nOutside work, I suppose the same pattern can bleed into personal time, because I’m still mentally chasing those details instead of being fully present. But I’m not sure I can give you a clean personal example without overstating it. Is that okay?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe a time when these attention issues interfered with your ability to maintain a conversation, complete a personal obligation, or interact effectively with someone close to you, even when work wasn’t involved?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one that isn’t really work-related, because I know you’re asking about people close to me or personal obligations.\n\nThe honest answer is… the strongest examples still happen around those meetings or calls. Like, I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and I should be following what someone is saying, but then I notice something tiny—a pump vibration, or a student making some side comment, or I remember an odd peak from an old spectrum—and suddenly I’m just gone mentally. I’ll chase that thread for hours afterward, and the actual conversation or obligation, the thing people needed me to stay with, just sort of falls apart.\n\nI don’t mean that I’m trying to ignore anyone. It’s more like my attention gets yanked away before I’ve really decided to let it happen. I know that’s not a perfect personal example, but it is the way it interferes with interacting with people. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situations outside of work—like with family, friends, or personal tasks—where your attention issues have caused real problems, even if work wasn’t involved?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re asking outside of work, and I don’t want to dodge the question. I’ll chase that one detail for hours, and meanwhile the thing everyone actually needed from me falls apart.\n\nSo with family or friends, it’s less like one clean incident I can point to and more that I’m not really there afterward. I know that may not be exactly what you’re asking, but that’s the honest version.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, have you noticed any situations where your attention issues have interfered with maintaining friendships, family relationships, or participating in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I feel like I keep circling back to the same thing. Outside of work specifically, I don’t have a neat example where I missed a dinner or lost a friendship because of it.\n\nWhat I can say is that it follows me out of the lab. I came in because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I had friends, at least at different points. I wasn’t completely isolated or anything.\n\nI do remember sometimes feeling a little… out of step, maybe. Like I was better when there was a shared class project or a topic we were all focused on, and less sure what to do in more casual situations. But I don’t know if that was really unusual, or just being a bit shy or serious.\n\nI wouldn’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it was. I guess I’m not sure how to judge that looking back.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if you often felt more comfortable engaging with others only when there was a shared activity or specific topic, rather than in more open or casual social situations?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe sometimes, yes, but I’m not sure I’d say “only.” That sounds a little stronger than how I remember it.\n\nI was probably more comfortable when there was something to do or talk about—like a class, a project, a specific subject—because then I knew what was expected of me. Casual situations could feel more vague, I guess. But I don’t know if that was unusual, or just that I liked having some structure.\n\nDoes that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these difficulties with focus or getting absorbed in details also affect your daily life outside of work, such as managing household tasks, relationships, or personal responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I guess it does, yes… though the main place I’ve noticed it is work, because that’s where the consequences are most obvious.\n\nLike, I came in because I can’t really keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I’ll notice something small—a pump vibration, or a student making some side comment, or some odd peak in an old spectrum—and then suddenly I’m off chasing that for hours. And the thing everyone actually needed from me just… doesn’t get done, or it falls apart.\n\nI don’t mean I’m trying to be careless. It feels like my attention gets pulled so hard by the detail that I almost convince myself it’s important. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of “daily life” effect you mean, but it’s definitely affecting what I’m responsible for.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you forget everyday things, struggle to manage your time, or have trouble keeping up with personal responsibilities like chores, appointments, or relationships, even when you’re not at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably does, though I notice it most clearly in work situations.\n\nIt’s less like I just forget a chore outright, and more like my mind gets pulled away from what I’m supposed to be doing. Like I said, I came in because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, notice something small—a pump vibration, a student’s side comment, some odd peak in an old spectrum—and then I’m off chasing that for hours. Meanwhile the thing people actually needed from me just falls apart.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s still a work example. It’s just the cleanest one I can explain. I guess the same kind of thing can make me lose track of normal responsibilities too, but I don’t want to overstate it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Looking back, would you say these tendencies to lose track of time, get absorbed in details, and have trouble staying on task started early in your life—like in childhood—or have they mostly developed more recently, in adulthood?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it goes all the way back to childhood, no. At least, not in any way I can clearly remember.\n\nThis has been more recent… about seven months, I think. It started after I took over the multi-site spectroscopy project. I don’t want to make it sound like I was perfectly organized before that, but this particular losing time and getting pulled into details really became noticeable around then.\n\nIs that the kind of timeline you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe any situations outside of work where these attention issues have caused problems—like in personal relationships, hobbies, or other activities that used to be important to you?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a good example outside of work, and I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is… but honestly the clearest examples are still tied to work.\n\nIt’s like during a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll catch some tiny thing—a pump vibration, a student’s side comment, or an odd peak in an old spectrum—and then my brain just locks onto it. I’ll follow that thread for hours, and meanwhile the main thing I was supposed to be doing, the thing people were counting on, sort of falls apart.\n\nOutside work, I suppose the same pattern can bleed into personal time, because I’m still mentally chasing those details instead of being fully present. But I’m not sure I can give you a clean personal example without overstating it. Is that okay?\n\nDoctor: Can you describe a time when these attention issues interfered with your ability to maintain a conversation, complete a personal obligation, or interact effectively with someone close to you, even when work wasn’t involved?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one that isn’t really work-related, because I know you’re asking about people close to me or personal obligations.\n\nThe honest answer is… the strongest examples still happen around those meetings or calls. Like, I’ll be in a grant call or a safety meeting, and I should be following what someone is saying, but then I notice something tiny—a pump vibration, or a student making some side comment, or I remember an odd peak from an old spectrum—and suddenly I’m just gone mentally. I’ll chase that thread for hours afterward, and the actual conversation or obligation, the thing people needed me to stay with, just sort of falls apart.\n\nI don’t mean that I’m trying to ignore anyone. It’s more like my attention gets yanked away before I’ve really decided to let it happen. I know that’s not a perfect personal example, but it is the way it interferes with interacting with people. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situations outside of work—like with family, friends, or personal tasks—where your attention issues have caused real problems, even if work wasn’t involved?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re asking outside of work, and I don’t want to dodge the question. I think… the problem is that the examples that feel “real” all start at work and then sort of take over everything else.\n\nI came in because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. Like, during a grant call or a safety meeting, I’ll notice a pump vibration, or a student says something off to the side, or I remember some strange peak in an old spectrum, and then I’m gone. I’ll chase that one detail for hours, and meanwhile the thing everyone actually needed from me falls apart.\n\nSo with family or friends, it’s less like one clean incident I can point to and more that I’m not really there afterward. My mind is still on that thread. I know that may not be exactly what you’re asking, but that’s the honest version.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, have you noticed any situations where your attention issues have interfered with maintaining friendships, family relationships, or participating in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I feel like I keep circling back to the same thing. Outside of work specifically, I don’t have a neat example where I missed a dinner or lost a friendship because of it.\n\nWhat I can say is that it follows me out of the lab. I came in because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore.
